However, initial public support for the new administration substantially declined in subsequent months as a result of its apparent inability to solve the corruption problems that had plagued the country since its independence from the Soviet Union, along with the murders of several members of parliament. Large-scale protests against president Bakiyev took place in Bishkek in April and November 2006, with opposition leaders accusing the president of failing to live up to his election promises to reform the country's constitution and transfer many of his presidential powers to parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kyrgyzstan is also a member of the <a href="/wiki/Organization_for_Security_and_Cooperation_in_Europe" class="mw-redirect" title="Organization for Security and Cooperation in Europe">Organization for Security and Cooperation in Europe</a> (OSCE), a league of 57 participating states committed to peace, transparency, and the protection of human rights in Eurasia. As an OSCE participating state, Kyrgyzstan's international commitments are subject to monitoring under the mandate of the <a href="/wiki/U.S._Helsinki_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Helsinki Commission">U.S. Helsinki Commission</a>.
</p><p>In December 2008, the state-owned broadcast <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyz_Television" title="Kyrgyz Television">KTRK</a> announced that it would require prior submission of <a href="/wiki/Radio_Free_Europe/Radio_Liberty" title="Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty">Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty</a> programmes, which KTRK are required to retransmit according to a 2005 agreement.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> KTRK had stopped retransmitting RFE/RL programming in October 2008, a week after it failed to broadcast an RFE/RL programme called <i>Inconvenient Questions</i> which covered the October elections, claiming to have lost the missing material. President Bakiyev had criticised this programme in September 2008, while KTRK told RFE/RL that its programming was too negative. <a href="/wiki/Reporters_Without_Borders" title="Reporters Without Borders">Reporters Without Borders</a>, which ranks Kyrgyzstan 111th out of 173 countries on its <a href="/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index" class="mw-redirect" title="Press Freedom Index">Press Freedom Index</a>, strongly criticised the decision.
</p><p>On 3 February 2009, President Bakiyev announced the imminent closure of the <a href="/wiki/Manas_Air_Base" class="mw-redirect" title="Manas Air Base">Manas Air Base</a>, the only US military base remaining in Central Asia.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The closure was approved by Parliament on 19 February 2009 by a vote of 78–1 for the government-backed bill.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> However, after much behind-the-scenes negotiation between Kyrgyz, Russian and American diplomats, the decision was reversed in June 2009. The Americans were allowed to remain under a new contract, whereby rent would increase from $17.4 million to $60 million annually.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:SCO_summit_(2018-06-10)_7.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/SCO_summit_%282018-06-10%29_7.jpg/220px-SCO_summit_%282018-06-10%29_7.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="136" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/SCO_summit_%282018-06-10%29_7.jpg/330px-SCO_summit_%282018-06-10%29_7.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/SCO_summit_%282018-06-10%29_7.jpg/440px-SCO_summit_%282018-06-10%29_7.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1880" data-file-height="1160" /></a><figcaption>President Jeenbekov at the <a href="/wiki/Shanghai_Cooperation_Organisation" title="Shanghai Cooperation Organisation">Shanghai Cooperation Organisation</a> summit in China, June 2018</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kyrgyzstan is among the fifty countries in the world with the highest perceived level of corruption: the 2016 <a href="/wiki/Corruption_Perception_Index" class="mw-redirect" title="Corruption Perception Index">Corruption Perception Index</a> for Kyrgyzstan is 28 on a scale of 0 (most corrupt) to 100 (least corrupt).<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2010 another revolution erupted in the country (see: <a href="/wiki/2010_Kyrgyzstani_revolution" class="mw-redirect" title="2010 Kyrgyzstani revolution">April uprising</a>). President Bakiyev, together with his relatives, including his son <a href="/wiki/Maxim_Bakiyev" title="Maxim Bakiyev">Maksim</a><sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> and brother <a href="/wiki/Janish_Bakiyev" title="Janish Bakiyev">Janish</a>—were forced to flee to Kazakhstan and then sought asylum in Belarus. <a href="/wiki/Roza_Otunbayeva" title="Roza Otunbayeva">Roza Otunbayeva</a>, who was appointed interim president, announced that she did not intend to run for the <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyz_presidential_election,_2011" class="mw-redirect" title="Kyrgyz presidential election, 2011">Presidential elections in 2011</a>. The election was held in November and won by Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Almazbek_Atambayev" title="Almazbek Atambayev">Almazbek Atambayev</a>, leader of the <a href="/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_of_Kyrgyzstan" title="Social Democratic Party of Kyrgyzstan">Social Democratic Party</a>, and Atambayev was sworn in as president on 1 December 2011. <a href="/wiki/Omurbek_Babanov" class="mw-redirect" title="Omurbek Babanov">Omurbek Babanov</a> was appointed prime minister on the same day and was confirmed on 23 December 2011.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2015 Kyrgyzstan became a full-fledged member of the <a href="/wiki/Eurasian_Economic_Union" title="Eurasian Economic Union">Eurasian Economic Union</a> (EES) after it formally abolished customs controls along its border with Kazakhstan, other members are the former Soviet republics Russia, Kazakhstan, <a href="/wiki/Belarus" title="Belarus">Belarus</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Armenia" title="Armenia">Armenia</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2023_Moscow_Victory_Day_Parade_01.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/2023_Moscow_Victory_Day_Parade_01.jpg/220px-2023_Moscow_Victory_Day_Parade_01.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="136" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/2023_Moscow_Victory_Day_Parade_01.jpg/330px-2023_Moscow_Victory_Day_Parade_01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/2023_Moscow_Victory_Day_Parade_01.jpg/440px-2023_Moscow_Victory_Day_Parade_01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1880" data-file-height="1160" /></a><figcaption>President Japarov (right) with <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Putin" title="Vladimir Putin">Vladimir Putin</a> and other post-Soviet leaders at the <a href="/wiki/2023_Moscow_Victory_Day_Parade" title="2023 Moscow Victory Day Parade">2023 Moscow Victory Day Parade</a><sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>In October 2017, <a href="/wiki/Sooronbay_Jeenbekov" title="Sooronbay Jeenbekov">Sooronbay Jeenbekov</a>, a former prime minister backed by incumbent Almazbek Atambayev, was elected as the new President of Kyrgyzstan.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> In foreign policy he saw the <a href="/wiki/Kremlin" title="Kremlin">Kremlin</a> as the country's "main strategic partner" and <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">China</a> as an "important strategic and trade partner", but he intended to seek more collaborative bilateral ties with European partners.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> On 7 August 2019, the Special Forces of Kyrgyzstan launched an operation against the residence of former President Almazbek Atambayev, supposedly based on charges of corruption made against him.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> In a meeting of the <a href="/wiki/Security_Council_of_Kyrgyzstan" title="Security Council of Kyrgyzstan">Security Council</a>, President Jeenbekov accused Atambayev of violating the constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> In October 2020, President Sooronbay Jeenbekov resigned after protests caused by irregularities in parliamentary <a href="/wiki/2020_Kyrgyz_parliamentary_election" title="2020 Kyrgyz parliamentary election">elections</a> on 4 October 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 2021, <a href="/wiki/Sadyr_Japarov" title="Sadyr Japarov">Sadyr Japarov</a> was elected as the new president after winning the presidential <a href="/wiki/2021_Kyrgyz_presidential_election" title="2021 Kyrgyz presidential election">election</a> by a landslide.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 2021, the majority of voters approved in the constitutional <a href="/wiki/2021_Kyrgyz_constitutional_referendum" title="2021 Kyrgyz constitutional referendum">referendum</a> a new constitution that will give new powers to the president, significantly strengthening the power of the presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Administrative_divisions">Administrative divisions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kyrgyzstan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Administrative divisions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Kyrgyzstan" title="Regions of Kyrgyzstan">Regions of Kyrgyzstan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Districts_of_Kyrgyzstan" title="Districts of Kyrgyzstan">Districts of Kyrgyzstan</a></div>
<p>Kyrgyzstan is divided into seven <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Kyrgyzstan" title="Regions of Kyrgyzstan">regions</a> (<a href="/wiki/Kyrgyz_language" title="Kyrgyz language">Kyrgyz</a>: <span lang="ky">облустар</span>). The regions are subdivided into 44 <a href="/wiki/Districts_of_Kyrgyzstan" title="Districts of Kyrgyzstan">districts</a> (<a href="/wiki/Kyrgyz_language" title="Kyrgyz language">Kyrgyz</a>: <span lang="ky">аймактар</span>, <span title="Kyrgyz-language text"><i lang="ky-Latn">aymaqtar</i></span>;). The districts are further subdivided into rural districts at the lowest level of administration, which include all rural settlements (<i>aýyl ökmötü</i>) and villages without an associated municipal government.
</p><p><span class="anchor" id="special_status_cities"></span>The cities of <a href="/wiki/Bishkek" title="Bishkek">Bishkek</a> and <a href="/wiki/Osh" title="Osh">Osh</a> have status "state importance" and do not belong to any region.
</p><p>Each region is headed by an akim (regional governor) appointed by the president. District akims are appointed by regional akims.
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right noresize mw-ext-imagemap-desc-bottom-left" typeof="mw:File"><span title="A clickable map of Kyrgyzstan exhibiting its provinces."><img alt="A clickable map of Kyrgyzstan exhibiting its provinces." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Kyrgyzstan_provinces_map.png/400px-Kyrgyzstan_provinces_map.png" decoding="async" width="400" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Kyrgyzstan_provinces_map.png/600px-Kyrgyzstan_provinces_map.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/6d/Kyrgyzstan_provinces_map.png 2x" data-file-width="750" data-file-height="423" usemap="#ImageMap_10b16ea57b740663" resource="/wiki/File:Kyrgyzstan_provinces_map.png" /></span><map name="ImageMap_10b16ea57b740663"><area href="/wiki/Osh" shape="rect" coords="114,120,130,137" alt="Osh" title="Osh" /><area href="/wiki/Bishkek" shape="rect" coords="209,10,224,25" alt="Bishkek" title="Bishkek" /><area href="/wiki/Batken_Region" shape="rect" coords="51,166,75,186" alt="Batken Region" title="Batken Region" /><area href="/wiki/Osh_Region" shape="rect" coords="141,150,161,171" alt="Osh Region" title="Osh Region" /><area href="/wiki/Jalal-Abad_Region" shape="rect" coords="116,80,140,99" alt="Jalal-Abad Region" title="Jalal-Abad Region" /><area href="/wiki/Naryn_Region" shape="rect" coords="213,89,240,109" alt="Naryn Region" title="Naryn Region" /><area href="/wiki/Talas_Region" shape="rect" coords="107,37,128,58" alt="Talas Region" title="Talas Region" /><area href="/wiki/Ch%C3%BCy_Region" shape="rect" coords="172,31,187,48" alt="Chüy Region" title="Chüy Region" /><area href="/wiki/Issyk-Kul_Region" shape="rect" coords="297,53,318,73" alt="Issyk-Kul Region" title="Issyk-Kul Region" /></map><figcaption>A clickable map of Kyrgyzstan exhibiting its provinces.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The regions, and independent cities, are as follows, with subdivisions:
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military">Military</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kyrgyzstan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Military">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_the_Republic_of_Kyrgyzstan" class="mw-redirect" title="Armed Forces of the Republic of Kyrgyzstan">Armed Forces of the Republic of Kyrgyzstan</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Multinational_forces_participate_in_Regional_Cooperation_12_Exercise_-06.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Multinational_forces_participate_in_Regional_Cooperation_12_Exercise_-06.JPG/220px-Multinational_forces_participate_in_Regional_Cooperation_12_Exercise_-06.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Multinational_forces_participate_in_Regional_Cooperation_12_Exercise_-06.JPG/330px-Multinational_forces_participate_in_Regional_Cooperation_12_Exercise_-06.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Multinational_forces_participate_in_Regional_Cooperation_12_Exercise_-06.JPG/440px-Multinational_forces_participate_in_Regional_Cooperation_12_Exercise_-06.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4256" data-file-height="2832" /></a><figcaption>Kyrgyz soldiers conducting mine sweeping exercises</figcaption></figure>
<p>The armed forces of Kyrgyzstan were formed after the <a href="/wiki/Collapse_of_the_Soviet_Union" class="mw-redirect" title="Collapse of the Soviet Union">collapse of the Soviet Union</a> and consist of the <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyz_Army" title="Kyrgyz Army">Land Forces</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyz_Air_Force" title="Kyrgyz Air Force">Air Forces</a>, <a href="/wiki/Internal_Troops_of_Kyrgyzstan" class="mw-redirect" title="Internal Troops of Kyrgyzstan">internal troops</a>, <a href="/wiki/National_Guard_(Kyrgyzstan)" title="National Guard (Kyrgyzstan)">National Guard</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyzstan_Frontier_Force" class="mw-redirect" title="Kyrgyzstan Frontier Force">border guard</a>. The military works with the <a href="/wiki/US_Armed_Forces" class="mw-redirect" title="US Armed Forces">US Armed Forces</a>, which leased a facility named the <a href="/wiki/Transit_Center_at_Manas" title="Transit Center at Manas">Transit Center at Manas</a> at Manas International Airport near Bishkek until June 2014.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> In recent years, the armed forces have begun developing better relations with Russia including signing modernization deals worth $1.1bn and participating in more exercises with Russian troops.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/w/index.php?title=Agency_of_National_Security_(Kyrgyzstan)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Agency of National Security (Kyrgyzstan) (page does not exist)">Agency of National Security</a> works with the military and serves similar purposes to its Soviet predecessor, the <a href="/wiki/KGB" title="KGB">KGB</a>. It oversees an elite counterterrorism special forces unit known as "Alfa", the same name used by other former Soviet countries, including Russia and <a href="/wiki/Uzbekistan" title="Uzbekistan">Uzbekistan</a>. The police are commanded by the Ministry of the Interior Affairs, along with the border guard.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Human_rights">Human rights</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kyrgyzstan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Human rights">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Human_rights_in_Kyrgyzstan" title="Human rights in Kyrgyzstan">Human rights in Kyrgyzstan</a></div>
<p>Kyrgyzstan is classified as a "<a href="/wiki/Hybrid_regime" title="Hybrid regime">hybrid regime</a>" in the <a href="/wiki/Democracy_Index" class="mw-redirect" title="Democracy Index">Democracy Index</a>, ranking 107th out of 167 for 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Kyrgyzstan was also ranked "not free" in the 2021 <a href="/wiki/Freedom_in_the_World" title="Freedom in the World">Freedom in the World</a> report with a score of 28/100. In 2020, it was ranked "partly free" with a score of 39/100.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the installment of a more democratic government, many human rights violations still take place. In a move that alarmed human-rights groups, dozens of prominent Uzbek religious and community leaders were arrested by security forces following the <a href="/wiki/2010_South_Kyrgyzstan_ethnic_clashes" title="2010 South Kyrgyzstan ethnic clashes">2010 South Kyrgyzstan riots</a>, including journalist and human-rights activist <a href="/wiki/Azimzhan_Askarov" title="Azimzhan Askarov">Azimzhan Askarov</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Kramer_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kramer-98">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> A law banning women under the age of 23 from traveling abroad without a parent or guardian, with the purpose of "increased morality and preservation of the gene pool" passed in the Kyrgyz parliament in June 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> American diplomats expressed concern in October 2014 when Kyrgyzstan lawmakers passed a law that imposes jail terms on <a href="/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Kyrgyzstan" title="LGBT rights in Kyrgyzstan">gay-rights activists</a> and others, including journalists, who create "a positive attitude toward non-traditional sexual relations."<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kyrgyzstani activist and journalist Azimzhan Askarov was sentenced to life in prison in 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> On 24 January 2017, a Kyrgyz court has reinstated a sentence of life imprisonment for Askarov.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kyrgyzstan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Kyrgyzstan" title="Economy of Kyrgyzstan">Economy of Kyrgyzstan</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kyrgyzstan_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Kyrgyzstan_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/290px-Kyrgyzstan_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="290" height="189" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Kyrgyzstan_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/435px-Kyrgyzstan_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Kyrgyzstan_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/580px-Kyrgyzstan_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1100" data-file-height="715" /></a><figcaption>A proportional representation of Kyrgyzstan exports, 2019</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/National_Bank_of_the_Kyrgyz_Republic" title="National Bank of the Kyrgyz Republic">National Bank of the Kyrgyz Republic</a> serves as the <a href="/wiki/Central_bank" title="Central bank">central bank</a> of Kyrgyzstan.
</p><p>Kyrgyzstan was the ninth poorest country in the former <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a>, and is today the second poorest country in <a href="/wiki/Central_Asia" title="Central Asia">Central Asia</a> after <a href="/wiki/Tajikistan" title="Tajikistan">Tajikistan</a>. 22.4% of the country's population lives below the poverty line.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite the backing of major Western lenders, including the <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">International Monetary Fund</a> (IMF), the <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Asian_Development_Bank" title="Asian Development Bank">Asian Development Bank</a>, Kyrgyzstan has had economic difficulties following independence. Initially, these were a result of the breakup of the Soviet trade bloc and resulting loss of markets, which impeded the republic's transition to a demand economy.
</p><p>The government has reduced expenditures, ended most price subsidies and introduced a <a href="/wiki/Value-added_tax" title="Value-added tax">value-added tax</a>. Overall, the government appears committed to the transition to a <a href="/wiki/Market_economy" title="Market economy">market economy</a>. Through economic stabilization and reform, the government seeks to establish a pattern of long-term consistent growth. Reforms led to Kyrgyzstan's accession to the <a href="/wiki/World_Trade_Organization" title="World Trade Organization">World Trade Organization</a> (WTO) on 20 December 1998.
</p><p>The Kyrgyz economy was severely affected by the collapse of the Soviet Union and the resulting loss of its vast market. In 1990, some 98% of Kyrgyz exports went to other parts of the Soviet Union. Thus, the nation's economic performance in the early 1990s was worse than any other former Soviet republic except war-torn <a href="/wiki/Armenia" title="Armenia">Armenia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Azerbaijan" title="Azerbaijan">Azerbaijan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tajikistan" title="Tajikistan">Tajikistan</a>, as factories and state farms collapsed with the disappearance of their traditional markets in the former Soviet Union. While economic performance has improved considerably in the last few years, and particularly since 1998, difficulties remain in securing adequate <a href="/wiki/Fiscal_Policy" class="mw-redirect" title="Fiscal Policy">fiscal</a> revenues and providing an adequate <a href="/wiki/Social_safety_net" title="Social safety net">social safety net</a>. <a href="/wiki/Remittance" title="Remittance">Remittances</a> of around 800,000 Kyrgyz migrants working in Russia contribute to the economy however in recent years, remittances have decreased.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Agriculture" title="Agriculture">Agriculture</a> is an important sector of the economy in Kyrgyzstan (see <a href="/wiki/Agriculture_in_Kyrgyzstan" title="Agriculture in Kyrgyzstan">agriculture in Kyrgyzstan</a>). By the early 1990s, the private agricultural sector provided between one-third and one-half of some harvests. In 2002, agriculture accounted for 35.6% of GDP and about half of employment. Kyrgyzstan's terrain is mountainous, which accommodates <a href="/wiki/Livestock" title="Livestock">livestock</a> raising, the largest agricultural activity, so the resulting <a href="/wiki/Wool" title="Wool">wool</a>, <a href="/wiki/Meat" title="Meat">meat</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dairy_product" title="Dairy product">dairy products</a> are major commodities. Main crops include <a href="/wiki/Wheat" title="Wheat">wheat</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sugar_beets" class="mw-redirect" title="Sugar beets">sugar beets</a>, <a href="/wiki/Potatoes" class="mw-redirect" title="Potatoes">potatoes</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cotton" title="Cotton">cotton</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tobacco" title="Tobacco">tobacco</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vegetables" class="mw-redirect" title="Vegetables">vegetables</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Fruit" title="Fruit">fruit</a>. As the prices of imported <a href="/wiki/Agrichemicals" class="mw-redirect" title="Agrichemicals">agrichemicals</a> and <a href="/wiki/Petroleum" title="Petroleum">petroleum</a> are so high, much farming is being done by hand and by horse, as it was generations ago. Agricultural processing is a key component of the industrial economy as well as one of the most attractive sectors for foreign investment.
</p><p>Kyrgyzstan is rich in mineral resources but has negligible <a href="/wiki/Petroleum" title="Petroleum">petroleum</a> and <a href="/wiki/Natural_gas" title="Natural gas">natural gas</a> reserves; it imports petroleum and gas. Among its mineral reserves are substantial deposits of <a href="/wiki/Coal" title="Coal">coal</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gold" title="Gold">gold</a>, <a href="/wiki/Uranium" title="Uranium">uranium</a>, <a href="/wiki/Antimony" title="Antimony">antimony</a>, and other valuable metals. <a href="/wiki/Metallurgy" title="Metallurgy">Metallurgy</a> is an important industry, and the government hopes to attract foreign investment in this field. The government has actively encouraged foreign involvement in extracting and processing gold from the <a href="/wiki/Kumtor_Gold_Mine" title="Kumtor Gold Mine">Kumtor Gold Mine</a> and other regions. The country's plentiful water resources and mountainous terrain enable it to produce and export large quantities of <a href="/wiki/Hydroelectric_energy" class="mw-redirect" title="Hydroelectric energy">hydroelectric energy</a>.
</p><p>The principal exports are nonferrous metals and minerals, woollen goods and other agricultural products, electric energy and certain engineering goods. Imports include petroleum and natural gas, ferrous metals, chemicals, most machinery, wood and paper products, some foods and some construction materials. Its leading trade partners include Germany, Russia, China, <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan" title="Kazakhstan">Kazakhstan</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Uzbekistan" title="Uzbekistan">Uzbekistan</a>. After Beijing launched the Belt and Road Initiative (BRI) in 2013, China has expanded its economic presence and initiated a number of sizable infrastructure projects in Kyrgyzstan.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In regards to telecommunication infrastructure, Kyrgyz Republic ranks last in Central Asia in the World Economic Forum's Network Readiness Index (NRI)—an indicator for determining the development level of a country's information and communication technologies. Kyrgyz Republic ranked number 118 overall in the 2014 NRI ranking, unchanged from 2013 (see <a href="/wiki/Networked_Readiness_Index" title="Networked Readiness Index">Networked Readiness Index</a>).
</p><p>Kyrgyzstan is ranked 78th among countries for economic freedom by the Heritage Institute.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The COVID-19 pandemic is expected to have a significant negative impact on the Kyrgyz economy that is reliant on services, remittances and natural resources. As a result, in order to mitigate the economic shock and preserve much of the development progress achieved in recent years the World Bank will provide support by financing several projects in the country.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tourism">Tourism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kyrgyzstan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Tourism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Issyk-Kulmeer.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Issyk-Kulmeer.jpg/220px-Issyk-Kulmeer.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Issyk-Kulmeer.jpg/330px-Issyk-Kulmeer.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Issyk-Kulmeer.jpg/440px-Issyk-Kulmeer.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="427" /></a><figcaption>Southern shore of <a href="/wiki/Issyk_Kul_Lake" class="mw-redirect" title="Issyk Kul Lake">Issyk Kul Lake</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Osh_Bazar.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Osh_Bazar.jpg/220px-Osh_Bazar.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Osh_Bazar.jpg/330px-Osh_Bazar.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Osh_Bazar.jpg/440px-Osh_Bazar.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1536" /></a><figcaption>Bazar in <a href="/wiki/Osh" title="Osh">Osh</a></figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Tourism_in_Kyrgyzstan" title="Tourism in Kyrgyzstan">Tourism in Kyrgyzstan</a></div>
<p>One of the most popular tourist destination points in Kyrgyzstan is the lake <a href="/wiki/Issyk-Kul" title="Issyk-Kul">Issyk-Kul</a>. Numerous hotels, resorts and boarding houses are located along its northern shore. The most popular beach zones are in the city of <a href="/wiki/Cholpon-Ata" title="Cholpon-Ata">Cholpon-Ata</a> and the settlements nearby, such as Kara-Oi (Dolinka), Bosteri and Korumdy. The number of tourists visiting the lake was more than a million a year in 2006 and 2007. However, due to the economic and political instability in the region, the number has declined in recent years.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Science_and_technology">Science and technology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kyrgyzstan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Science and technology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Kyrgyzstan" title="Science and technology in Kyrgyzstan">Science and technology in Kyrgyzstan</a></div>
<p>The headquarters of the Kyrgyz Academy of Sciences is located in Bishkek, where several research institutes are located. Kyrgyz researchers are developing useful technologies based on natural products, such as heavy metal remediation for purifying waste water.<sup id="cite_ref-Yurishcheva_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Yurishcheva-110">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Kyrgyzstan was ranked 98th in the <a href="/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index" title="Global Innovation Index">Global Innovation Index</a> in 2021, down from 90th in 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kyrgyzstan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Demographics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pyramide_Kirghizistan.PNG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Pyramide_Kirghizistan.PNG/350px-Pyramide_Kirghizistan.PNG" decoding="async" width="350" height="243" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Pyramide_Kirghizistan.PNG/525px-Pyramide_Kirghizistan.PNG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Pyramide_Kirghizistan.PNG/700px-Pyramide_Kirghizistan.PNG 2x" data-file-width="902" data-file-height="625" /></a><figcaption>A <a href="/wiki/Population_pyramid" title="Population pyramid">population pyramid</a> showing Kyrgyzstan's age distribution (2005)</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:%D0%9D%D0%B0%D1%81%D0%B5%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%B5_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D0%B8%D0%B8_%D0%B7%D0%B0_2015.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/%D0%9D%D0%B0%D1%81%D0%B5%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%B5_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D0%B8%D0%B8_%D0%B7%D0%B0_2015.png/350px-%D0%9D%D0%B0%D1%81%D0%B5%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%B5_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D0%B8%D0%B8_%D0%B7%D0%B0_2015.png" decoding="async" width="350" height="189" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/%D0%9D%D0%B0%D1%81%D0%B5%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%B5_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D0%B8%D0%B8_%D0%B7%D0%B0_2015.png/525px-%D0%9D%D0%B0%D1%81%D0%B5%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%B5_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D0%B8%D0%B8_%D0%B7%D0%B0_2015.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/%D0%9D%D0%B0%D1%81%D0%B5%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%B5_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D0%B8%D0%B8_%D0%B7%D0%B0_2015.png/700px-%D0%9D%D0%B0%D1%81%D0%B5%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%B5_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D0%B8%D0%B8_%D0%B7%D0%B0_2015.png 2x" data-file-width="1900" data-file-height="1028" /></a><figcaption>Population density of Kyrgyzstan, 2015<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Kyrgyzstan" title="Demographics of Kyrgyzstan">Demographics of Kyrgyzstan</a></div>
<p>Kyrgyzstan's population is estimated at 6,586,600 in August 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-census_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-census-116">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> Of those, 34.4% are under the age of 15 and 6.2% are over 65. The country is <a href="/wiki/Rural" class="mw-redirect" title="Rural">rural</a>: only about one-third of the population live in urban areas. The average <a href="/wiki/Population_density" title="Population density">population density</a> is 25 people per km<sup>2</sup>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ethnic_groups">Ethnic groups</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kyrgyzstan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Ethnic groups">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The nation's largest <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_group" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethnic group">ethnic group</a> are the <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyz_people" title="Kyrgyz people">Kyrgyz</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Turkic_peoples" title="Turkic peoples">Turkic</a> people, who comprise 74.1% of the population. Other ethnic groups include <a href="/wiki/Russians" title="Russians">Russians</a> (5.0%) concentrated in the north and <a href="/wiki/Uzbeks" title="Uzbeks">Uzbeks</a> (14.8%) living in the south. Small but noticeable minorities include <a href="/wiki/Dungans" class="mw-redirect" title="Dungans">Dungans</a> (1.1%), <a href="/wiki/Uyghur_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Uyghur people">Uyghurs</a> (0.9%), <a href="/wiki/T%C4%81jik_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Tājik people">Tajiks</a> (0.9%), <a href="/wiki/Kazakhs" title="Kazakhs">Kazakhs</a> (0.6%), and <a href="/wiki/Ukrainians" title="Ukrainians">Ukrainians</a> (0.1%) and other smaller ethnic minorities.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> The country has over 80 ethnic groups.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Kyrgyz have historically been semi-<a href="/wiki/Nomad" title="Nomad">nomadic</a> herders, living in round tents called <a href="/wiki/Yurt" title="Yurt">yurts</a> and tending <a href="/wiki/Sheep" title="Sheep">sheep</a>, <a href="/wiki/Horse" title="Horse">horses</a> and <a href="/wiki/Yak" title="Yak">yaks</a>. This nomadic tradition continues to function seasonally (see <a href="/wiki/Transhumance" title="Transhumance">transhumance</a>) as herding families return to the high mountain pasture (or <i>jailoo</i>) in the summer. The sedentary Uzbeks and Tajiks traditionally have farmed lower-lying irrigated land in the <a href="/wiki/Fergana_valley" class="mw-redirect" title="Fergana valley">Fergana valley</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kyrgyzstan has undergone a pronounced change in its ethnic composition since independence.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-spo_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-spo-122">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-spoo_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-spoo-123">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> The percentage of ethnic Kyrgyz has increased from around 50% in 1979 to over 70% in 2013, while the percentage of ethnic groups, such as Russians, Ukrainians, Germans and Tatars dropped from 35% to about 7%.<sup id="cite_ref-census_116-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-census-116">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> Since 1991, a large number of <a href="/wiki/Germans_in_Kyrgyzstan" class="mw-redirect" title="Germans in Kyrgyzstan">Germans</a>, who in 1989 numbered 101,000 persons, have emigrated to Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kyrgyzstan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Languages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Kyrgyzstan" title="Languages of Kyrgyzstan">Languages of Kyrgyzstan</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kyrgyz_traditionalscript_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Kyrgyz_traditionalscript_2.jpg/220px-Kyrgyz_traditionalscript_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="123" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Kyrgyz_traditionalscript_2.jpg/330px-Kyrgyz_traditionalscript_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Kyrgyz_traditionalscript_2.jpg/440px-Kyrgyz_traditionalscript_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1920" data-file-height="1077" /></a><figcaption>The name of Kyrgyzstan rendered in the <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyz_alphabets" title="Kyrgyz alphabets">traditional script</a> in use from the 13th century to 1920</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Kyrgyz_language" title="Kyrgyz language">Kyrgyz</a> is the state language of Kyrgyzstan. <a href="/wiki/Russian_language" title="Russian language">Russian</a> is additionally an official language. 
Kyrgyzstan is one of five former Soviet republics to have Russian as a <i><a href="/wiki/De_jure" title="De jure">de jure</a></i> <a href="/wiki/Official_language" title="Official language">official language</a>, along with Russia, <a href="/wiki/Belarus" title="Belarus">Belarus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan" title="Kazakhstan">Kazakhstan</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Tajikistan" title="Tajikistan">Tajikistan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto1_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-131">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> After the division of the Soviet Union into countries, Kyrgyz was adopted as the "state language" of Kyrgyzstan in 1991. Kyrgyzstan adopted Russian as an "official language" in 1997. The languages have different legal statuses.
</p><p>Kyrgyz is a <a href="/wiki/Turkic_languages" title="Turkic languages">Turkic</a> language of the <a href="/wiki/Kipchak_languages" title="Kipchak languages">Kipchak</a> branch, closely related to <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_language" title="Kazakh language">Kazakh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Karakalpak_language" title="Karakalpak language">Karakalpak</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Nogay_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Nogay language">Nogay Tatar</a>. It was written in the <a href="/wiki/Arabic_alphabet" title="Arabic alphabet">Arabic alphabet</a> until the twentieth century. The <a href="/wiki/Latin_alphabet" title="Latin alphabet">Latin script</a> was introduced and adopted on Stalin's orders in 1928, and was subsequently replaced by <a href="/wiki/Cyrillic_script#Kyrgyz" title="Cyrillic script">Cyrillic script</a> in 1941.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> A reformed Perso-Arabic alphabet, created by the Kyrgyz intellectual and scientist <a href="/wiki/Kasym_Tynystanov" title="Kasym Tynystanov">Kasym Tynystanov</a> is the official script of the Kyrgyz language in the People's Republic of China.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> As a result of the pending language reform in neighboring Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan will be the only independent <a href="/wiki/Turkic_languages" title="Turkic languages">Turkic-speaking country</a> in a few years that exclusively uses the Cyrillic alphabet.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> In April 2023, Russia suspended dairy exports to Kyrgyzstan after the chairman of Kyrgyzstan's National Commission for the State Language and Language Policies, Kanybek Osmonaliev, proposed to change the official script from Cyrillic to Latin to bring the country in line with other Turkic-speaking nations. Osmonaliev was reprimanded by President <a href="/wiki/Sadyr_Japarov" title="Sadyr Japarov">Sadyr Japarov</a> who then clarified that Kyrgyzstan had no plans to replace the Cyrillic alphabet.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2009,<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> 4.1 million people spoke Kyrgyz as native or second language and 2.5 million spoke Russian as native or second language. <a href="/wiki/Uzbek_language" title="Uzbek language">Uzbek</a> is the second most common native language with 700,000 native speakers.
</p><p>Russian TV media enjoy enormous popularity in Kyrgyzstan, especially in the deeply <a href="/wiki/Russification" title="Russification">russified</a> <a href="/wiki/Bishkek" title="Bishkek">city of Bishkek</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Ch%C3%BCy_Region" title="Chüy Region">Chüy Region</a>, despite that the percentage of Russians today is a fraction of that in 1989. Russian media outlets have an enormous influence on public opinion in Kyrgyzstan, especially in areas such as <a href="/wiki/Human_rights" title="Human rights">human rights</a> and international political developments.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Many business and political affairs are carried out in Russian. Until recently, Kyrgyz remained a language spoken at home and was rarely used during meetings or other events. However, most parliamentary meetings today are conducted in Kyrgyz, with simultaneous interpretation available for those not speaking Kyrgyz. According to an <a href="/wiki/RFE/RL" class="mw-redirect" title="RFE/RL">RFE/RL</a> article from 2014, despite the attempts to raise the status of Kyrgyz, thousands of Kyrgyz are russifying their names every year (around 40,000), mostly for career prospects, and to remove themselves from the Russian blacklists (people who are to be deported upon entrance) by registering different names. There are also many Russian-language medium schools that are supported from the Russian foundations via the embassy of Russia in Bishkek which are better funded than the Kyrgyz language medium schools. Due to this, many ethnic Kyrgyz go to Russian language medium schools. Many high school students change their surnames annually; for example 800 such changes were recorded in high school students in the region of Naryn.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kyrgyzstan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
